    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{4\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, suggesting non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}---run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}---expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

    "newString": "\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}",
    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{6\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, suggesting non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}---run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}---expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

Still "contamination." at the top of page 9. The `-2pt` vspace and word change ("show" vs "exhibit") weren't enough. The problem is really stubborn — just one word overflows.

Let me think about this more carefully. The issue is that in two-column mode, LaTeX balances both columns. Page 8 has:
- Top: Figure 4 (full-width `figure*`)
- Left column: Figure 5 + beginning of Discussion §5.1
- Right column: Rest of §5.1 + §5.2 + §6 Conclusion

The `\enlargethispage` should allow both columns to grow. 

But the left column might not have enough content to fill the extended space, which prevents the right column from expanding too. I'm wondering if the real culprit is column balancing on the last page of main text — in ACL format, the final page typically tries to equalize columns, so LaTeX won't let one column extend further than the other.

Actually, page 8 isn't the document's last page since the appendix follows, so balancing shouldn't be happening. The section break between Conclusion and Limitations might be triggering something though. Let me try reducing the vertical spacing around figures on page 8 by adjusting the gap between figures and text, or even slightly shrinking figure 5 from its current dimensions to free up some vertical space.

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, suggesting non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}---run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}---expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

50	
51	\begin{figure*}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
54	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}
55	\label{fig:rho_degradation}
56	\end{figure*}
57	
58	\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}
59	\label{sec:groundtruth}
60	
61	\looseness=-2
62	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$; $p = 0.88$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Crucially, label-independent evidence confirms mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\chi^2 = 562.7$; \Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).
63	
64	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
65	\label{sec:structural}
66	
67	\looseness=-1
68	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
69	
70	
71	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
72	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
73	
74	\looseness=-1
75	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} \approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\% C\% (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\sim}$20\% (\Cref{app:method_details}).
76	
77	\begin{figure}[t]
78	\centering
79	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}
80	\caption{Cross-subject vs.\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}
81	\label{fig:matching}
82	\end{figure}
83	
84	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
```

    "oldString": "\\setlength{\\textfloatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 2pt}\n\\section{Discussion}",
    "newString": "\\section{Discussion}",
    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}\n\\setlength{\\textfloatsep}{6pt plus 1pt minus 2pt}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, suggesting non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}---run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}---DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}---expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

separation\"(introduction.tex:18)；\"confirming DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic\"(discussion.tex:8)。建议至少将主正文半数改为独立短句。\n- [info] [去AI] limitations.tex:9 出现一个 \"Additionally\"——单次使用可接受，但结合整体 -ing [飞书用户 用户]: ⚠️ 修正：论文中的仓库链接必须用匿名链接

之前发的消息里给的是 GitHub 仓库地址，但论文里不能直接用 GitHub 链接。GitHub 仓库即使 org 名叫 anonymous，reviewer 仍然可以通过 org 页面、成员列表等途径追溯身份，不符合匿名审稿要求。

---

你这个项目的两个链接：

  论文中使用（匿名链接）：
  https://anonymous.4open.science/r/irt_equated_bench_regen-2431/

  推送代码使用（GitHub 仓库）：
  https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/irt_equated_bench_regen

---

区分用途：

1. 论文脚注里的 \url{} 必须填匿名链接（anonymous.4open.science），这是 reviewer 看到的唯一入口
2. 日常 git push 代码、整理仓库、用 PAT 推送 —— 还是用 GitHub 仓库地址，这个不变
3. anonymous.4open.science 会自动从 GitHub 仓库同步，你只要保证 GitHub 仓库内容是最新的就行

请立刻检查论文：

1. 找到脚注里的仓库链接
2. 如果写的是 github.com 开头的地址，替换成上面的匿名链接
3. 确认脚注格式是之前修正过的标准格式（整句写在脚注里面，Abstract 原文不动）

正确示例：

... (Abstract 最后一句原文)\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/irt_equated_bench_regen-2431/}.}.

完成后请汇报：论文脚注中的链接是否已替换为匿名链接？

[PAPER AUDIT] irt_equated_bench_regen 论文质量审计报告

━━━ 确定性检查 ━━━
编译: ✅
页数: 17
源码: 5 critical, 153 warning

━━━ 内容结构 ━━━
- [critical] [叙事一致性] Conclusion 仅覆盖 Contribution 1（temporal DIF findings），完全未提及 Contribution 2（cross-subject matching）和 Contribution 3（Monitor/Triage/Correct 审计协议）。单句 Conclusion 断裂了 Intro→Conclusion 叙事弧线——审稿人会认为贡献列表与总结不匹配。(conclusion.tex:5)
- [critical] [叙事一致性] 论文在 29.2%（DEFF-corrected）和 31.3%（raw）之间反复切换，未始终标明使用的是哪个数字。Intro §1 写 "31%"，Contribution 1 写 "29.2%"，Fig 1 caption 写 "29.2% of items flagged as C"，但 Table 1 显示 C% = 31.3。审稿人在 Figure caption 和紧邻的 Table 之间交叉验证时会产生困惑。建议统一：正文以 raw 31.3% 为基准报告，DEFF 校正范围 [13.5%, 29.2%] 作为 bracketing，每次引用都明确标注。
- [warning] [叙事一致性] Abstract 写 "5,227 models across six benchmarks"，但不同 benchmark 模型数不同（MMLU=5,227, GSM8K=6,702）。"5,227 models across six benchmarks" 的括号结构暗示所有 benchmark 都是 5,227 个模型。建议改为 "5,227 models on MMLU, replicated across six benchmarks" 或类似限定表述。(abstract.tex:2)
- [warning] [去AI] 主正文含 11 处解释性 em-dash (---)：abstract×1, introduction×2, related_work×2, experiments×3, discussion×2, limitations×1。这是审稿人最敏感的 AI 写作标志。范例："temporal DIF---orders of magnitude above random baselines---while"(abstract.tex:2)；"model generations---measurement invariance---has not"(introduction.tex:7)。逐一改为逗号、括号或拆句。
- [warning] [去AI] ", confirming/revealing/providing" 分词结构在主正文出现约 8 次，Appendix 约 7 次。多数句子以 "..., confirming X" 收尾，形成公式化节奏。高频范例："revealing that global exposure and differential functioning are complementary"(abstract.tex:2)；"confirming mechanism separation"(introduction.tex:18)；"confirming DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic"(discussion.tex:8)。建议至少将主正文半数改为独立短句。
- [info] [去AI] limitations.tex:9 出现一个 "Additionally"——单次使用可接受，但结合整体 -ing 模式需注意。
- [warning] [Claim强度] Abstract 称 "orders of magnitude above random baselines"。实际比值范围为 45×–95×（Table 5），约 1.7 个数量级。"Orders of magnitude"（复数）通常暗示 ≥100×。建议改为 "an order of magnitude above" 或直接写 "45–95×"。(abstract.tex:2)
- [warning] [Claim强度] Introduction 称 "up to 851 rank positions"(introduction.tex:5)，但 Appendix §K (app:rank_reversal) 仅称 "exceed ±800 in extreme cases" 和 "±561 at the 99th percentile"。851 这个具体数字在正文任何表格或 appendix 中均未出现。定量 claim 必须可追溯到表格或明确数据引用。
- [warning] [Claim强度] Introduction 写 "addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation"(introduction.tex:16)。论文并未修复 wang2026fragility 指出的脆弱性——只是以此为动机。"Motivated by" 比 "addressing" 更准确。
- [warning] [贡献-范围] Contribution 2 将三个不同方法捆绑在一条中："cross-subject matching with ΔFPR=0 guarantee"（解决 matching 变量污染）+"DEFF correction"（解决模型家族非独立性）+"semi-synthetic validation"（验证检测力）。DEFF 与 cross-subject matching 解决的问题完全不同。审稿人可能认为贡献界定模糊。建议拆分或将 DEFF 降级为 robustness check。
- [warning] [贡献-范围] Contribution 3（audit protocol）在 Discussion §6.2 仅一段（约 80 words），对于一条独立贡献偏薄。建议增加 empirical grounding 的细节（如 threshold 选择依据、决策树），或降级为 Discussion 中的 practical implication 而非独立 contribution。
- [warning] [章节结构] Conclusion 仅一句话（~30 words），是异常简短的 Conclusion。缺乏对 implications、future directions、和审计协议的讨论。审稿人可能认为作者空间不够或反思不足。至少应覆盖三条贡献各一句 + 一句 broader impact。(conclusion.tex:5)
- [info] [章节结构] Introduction 未严格遵循 5 段结构（背景→问题→gap→方法→贡献）。现为 3 段文字 + figure + 贡献列表。第一段直接报告关键发现（31% DIF），在读者理解 gap 之前就给出了结果。虽然 "lead with the punchline" 在 measurement 类论文中可行，但可能让不熟悉 DIF 的审稿人失去 context。
- [warning] [符号] φ = 0.012 出现在 Abstract 和 Introduction(introduction.tex:7)，但在实际报告该分析的 Experiments §5.3 中缺失——§5.3 只报告了 Jaccard/enrichment/p-value。要么在 §5.3 补充 φ，要么从 Abstract/Intro 中移除。
- [info] [符号] ETS C 的称谓在正文中有 5 种变体："ETS C"、"Category C"、"ETS Category C"、"C-flagged"、"DIF-C"。虽非歧义，但增加了阅读认知负担。建议统一为 "ETS-C" 或 "Category C" + 在首次定义处确立。
- [info] [符号] Introduction §3 使用 "non-independence correction" 描述 DEFF 校正结果（29.2%），但 "design effect (DEFF)" 的定义直到稍后的 Contribution 2 才出现。读者在理解校正方法前就看到了校正后的数字。
- [info] [实验严谨性] 主结果 Table 1 (tab:temporal_landscape) 和 Table 5 (tab:cross_benchmark) 均无 error bars 或 CI 列。Bootstrap CI 在正文文字中提及但不在表格内，审稿人扫表时无法直接判断不确定性。建议至少在 Table 1 的 C% 列加 CI 或在 caption 中注明。
- [info] [可复现性] 论文未报告 MH-DIF 分析的计算成本（CPU hours / runtime）。虽然这不是训练论文，但在 5,227 models × 12,508 items + 多种 bootstrap + 多种 cohort 设计的规模下，计算成本信息有助于复现。
- [info] [章节过渡] Discussion §6.1 ("Temporal DIF as Structured Signal") 提供了高于 Results 的解读（base/instruct reversal 的机制讨论），不是简单重复数字。✓ 合格。
- [info] [章节过渡] Discussion §6.2 → Conclusion 过渡缺失：Discussion 以 "expert review, not automatic removal" 结束，Conclusion 直接跳到一句总结，没有任何 bridging。
- [info] [Limitations] 覆盖 3 个核心点（semi-synthetic 局限、independence 假设违反、cross-subject matching 边界条件），实质性好，未回避关键弱点。长度约 290 words / 2 段，符合 EMNLP 规则（"一小段，覆盖 3 个点"——虽偏长但合理）。
- [info] [内部信息] 未发现内部实验编号、服务器名、基础设施标识或不当内部流程引用。GitHub URL 使用匿名格式 (anonymous-nlp-2026-2)。✓ 合格。
- [info] [写作规则] Abstract 约 140 words，未超过 200 word 限制。✓ 合格。
- [info] [写作规则] Limitations section 仅一个，位于正文 Conclusion 之后、References 之前。Appendix 中无 Limitations section。✓ 合格。
- [info] [写作规则] ARR Responsible NLP Checklist 不在论文 PDF 中（由用户在 OpenReview 填写）。Appendix 有 "Use of AI Assistants" 声明。✓ 合格。
- [info] [审稿规则] 总页数 17 页。需确认正文（Abstract 到 Conclusion）≤ 8 页。从 .tex 结构看，Limitations 和 References 不计入限制，Appendix 不计入。建议编译后逐页核实 main text 部分不超过 8 页。

━━━ 视觉排版 ━━━
- [critical] [pages 1-9] [审稿规则] 主文（Abstract 至 Conclusion 结尾）延伸至第 9 页约 2/3 处，估计 ~8.5–8.7 页，超出 EMNLP 2026 严格的 8 页限制。第 8 页三张图（Figure 4/5/6）占约 90% 页面面积，是溢出的直接原因。需要将部分 figure 移至 appendix 或压缩正文以严格控制在 8 页内，否则有 desk rejection 风险。
- [warning] [page 8] 三张 figure（Figure 4 全宽 + Figure 5 + Figure 6）几乎占满整页，Section 5 "Discussion" 仅在页底有 2–3 行正文。图表过度集中导致视觉节奏失衡，且 Section 5 标题接近孤悬（orphan heading）。建议将 Figure 5 或 Figure 6 移至 appendix，既缓解密度问题又帮助满足页数限制。
- [warning] [page 8] Figure 4 包含 6 个子图，轴标签、刻度和子图标题字号明显小于正文（目测约 60–65% 正文字号），低于推荐的 ≥70% 阈值。印刷尺寸下可读性偏弱。建议增大子图尺寸或减少子图数量（如合并 benchmark 或选取代表性 3–4 个放 main text，其余移至 appendix）。
- [info] [page 1] 首页无 display item，仅有文字（Abstract + Introduction 开头）。第一个 display item（Figure 1 pipeline 图）出现在 page 2 顶部，位置可接受。若能将 Figure 1 提前到 page 1 底部或 Introduction 内嵌，可强化审稿人第一印象锚定。
- [info] [page 7] Table 2 约 9 列压缩在单栏宽度内，字号偏小但仍可辨认。若有空间可考虑改为 table* 全宽展示。
- [info] [page 17] 最后一页（appendix 末页）约 60% 有内容，底部 ~40% 空白。非主文问题，影响不大。

━━━ 总计 ━━━
Critical: 8 | Warning: 165 | Info: 17

===== 行动指引 =====

1. Critical 必须修：叙事漂移→统一全文叙事，占位符→立即清理，数字不一致→核对 registry，内部信息泄露→替换为描述性名称
2. Warning 建议修：去 AI 风格→批量修改，排版问题→调整 float/空白
3. Info 仅供参考

叙事不一致的论文比有 typo 的论文更难被接收。先统一叙事，再处理其他问题。

_source line 1150 · 2026-05-25T11:22:48.873Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DtKcEhQhHcfKnEQWhnShH3`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language model (LLM) benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models across six benchmarks), validated through semi-synthetic injection with a cross-subject matching guarantee. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 18--31\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF---orders of magnitude above random baselines---while aggregate rankings remain near-perfect ($\rho \geq 0.994$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap contamination labels, revealing that global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions, confirming structured capability shifts beyond contamination. We propose a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct audit protocol with empirically grounded thresholds for benchmark maintainers. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/irt_equated_bench_regen}.}.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TBzvbSZ1zzqzfJYS1AK2aa`</summary>

```text
1	\raggedbottom
2	\enlargethispage{-6\baselineskip}
3	\section*{Limitations}
4	
5	\looseness=-1
6	Three primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \emph{that} items function differently but not \emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\% CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\citep{french2010hierarchical}.
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	(3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp; \Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond multiple-choice question (MCQ) format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.
10	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	\looseness=-1
5	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\rho \approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 851 rank positions. This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.
6	
7	Existing approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}
12	\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \textbf{\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\ 2024 focal). \textbf{\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \textbf{\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \textbf{\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}
13	\label{fig:pipeline}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]
18	\item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
19	\item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
20	\item \textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```

{

  "old_string": "Large language model (LLM) benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models across six benchmarks), validated through semi-synthetic injection with a cross-subject matching guarantee. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 18--31\\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF---orders of magnitude above random baselines---while aggregate rankings remain near-perfect ($\\rho \\geq 0.994$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap contamination labels, revealing that global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions, confirming structured capability shifts beyond contamination. We propose a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct audit protocol with empirically grounded thresholds for benchmark maintainers. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/irt_equated_bench_regen-2431/}.}.",
  "new_string": "Large language model (LLM) benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models on MMLU, replicated across six benchmarks), validated through semi-synthetic injection with a cross-subject matching guarantee. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 18--31\\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF, 45--95$\\times$ above random baselines, while aggregate rankings remain near-perfect ($\\rho \\geq 0.994$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap contamination labels ($\\phi = 0.012$), indicating that global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions, confirming structured capability shifts beyond contamination. We propose a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct audit protocol with empirically grounded thresholds for benchmark maintainers. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/irt_equated_bench_regen-2431/}.}."

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "b843b6dc-a5a9-40a9-b81f-1a2bc27eb742",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "ec0b4c7a-0ecd-4a37-a056-22c89e4deff8",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "8e159252-1c5b-4c93-8614-4201f20002e2",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}.",
    "newString": "and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 851 rank positions. This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 13,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 13,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\label{fig:pipeline}",
          " \\end{figure*}",
          " ",
          "-We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addressing the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:",
          "+We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by up to 851 rank positions. This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations---\\emph{measurement invariance}---has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 29.2\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), confirming mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 15,

    "oldString": "yet it is rarely tested.     "newString": "yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected: scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} (measurement non-invariance across temporal cohorts, manifesting as score incomparability) as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected---scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by ${\\pm}561$ positions at the 99th percentile (\\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). This instability does not invalidate the benchmark---aggregate rankings are massively redundant---but it is diagnostic, revealing which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations (\\emph{measurement invariance}) has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\\% after DEFF correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

_source line 1198 · 2026-05-25T11:25:00.038Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014zaJCne7V8wPX2W4eoJYkM`</summary>

```text
1	\enlargethispage{8\baselineskip}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	
5	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-2
9	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---by design (\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\citep{camilli1994methods}, validating DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, suggesting non-contamination drivers (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).
10	
11	\looseness=-2
12	Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\% (29.2\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.
13	
14	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
15	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
16	
17	\looseness=-2
18	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\emph{Monitor}---run MH-DIF audits when ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}---DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}---expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
19	
```

</details>

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "559f66e4-0a41-4d1d-82bc-2b917538ed82",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}",
    "newString": "Result: 31.3\\% of items flagged as C (29.2\\% after DEFF correction), yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected: scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by ${\\pm}561$ positions at the 99th percentile (\\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). This instability does not invalidate the benchmark (aggregate rankings are massively redundant) but it is diagnostic: it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations (\\emph{measurement invariance}) has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\\% after DEFF correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 9,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 9,
        "newLines": 7,

          "-\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 29.2\\% of items flagged as C, yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}",

Result: 31.3\\% of items flagged as C (29.2\\% after DEFF correction), yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}",

          " ",
          " \\looseness=-2",
          "-Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$; $p = 0.88$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Crucially, label-independent evidence confirms mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$; \\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).",

    "oldString": "Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker",
    "newString": "Partial memorization scenarios (where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping) would produce weaker",
    "originalFile": "\\raggedbottom\n\\enlargethispage{-6\\baselineskip}\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios---where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping---would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \\emph{that} items function differently but not \\emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n(3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp; \\Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond multiple-choice question (MCQ) format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.\n",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Psychometric Methods for LLM Evaluation.}
5	\looseness=-1
6	The Mantel-Haenszel procedure \citep{holland1988mh} remains the most widely used DIF detector in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties, operating nonparametrically without requiring an explicit item response theory (IRT) model. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \citep{meredith1993invariance}, which examines whether an instrument functions equivalently across groups. IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated since \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced it to natural language processing: recent work calibrates test sets for discriminability, item selection, and adaptive evaluation. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses parametric IRT (two-parameter logistic [2PL] / three-parameter logistic [3PL] fixed-parameter calibration) to equate scores across temporal cohorts. MH-DIF is nonparametric and robust to model misspecification---an advantage given poor 2PL fit for MMLU (root mean square error of approximation [RMSEA] $> 0.08$; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}). Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected; our work diagnoses \emph{which} items exhibit instability and quantifies severity via ETS classification, providing pre-screened anchor candidates for downstream equating. Their anchor-item selection can directly consume our stable-item set; our finding that 31\% of items exhibit temporal DIF identifies which items should \emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability.
7	
8	\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}
9	\looseness=-1
10	A growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training-data analysis. Membership inference approaches use token-level log-likelihood statistics \citep{shi2024mink} or statistical anomalies \citep{dekoninck2024constat} to flag items likely seen during training. A standard validation practice is to compare detector output against web-overlap labels \citep{li2024contamination}, which classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}---whether an item exists in training data---rather than \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts. This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our comparison with web-overlap labels (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). \citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.
11	
12	\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}
13	\looseness=-1
14	Meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality~\citep{zhu2026bhi} assesses properties at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \emph{when} measurement properties shift. Work on LLM memorization \citep{carlini2022quantifying} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. MMLU-CF~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items without replacement; DIF monitoring identifies which existing items remain measurement-invariant. Dynamic benchmarks~\citep{kiela2021dynabench} mitigate contamination by construction but lack psychometric equating. Broader surveys~\citep{chang2024survey} identify benchmark saturation as a systemic challenge that DIF analysis directly addresses.
15

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Psychometric Methods for LLM Evaluation.}\n\\looseness=-1\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure \\citep{holland1988mh} remains the most widely used DIF detector in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties, operating nonparametrically without requiring an explicit item response theory (IRT) model. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance}, which examines whether an instrument functions equivalently across groups. IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated since \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced it to natural language processing: recent work calibrates test sets for discriminability, item selection, and adaptive evaluation. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses parametric IRT (two-parameter logistic [2PL] / three-parameter logistic [3PL] fixed-parameter calibration) to equate scores across temporal cohorts. MH-DIF is nonparametric and robust to model misspecification---an advantage given poor 2PL fit for MMLU (root mean square error of approximation [RMSEA] $> 0.08$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}). Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected; our work diagnoses \\emph{which} items exhibit instability and quantifies severity via ETS classification, providing pre-screened anchor candidates for downstream equating. Their anchor-item selection can directly consume our stable-item set; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF identifies which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\n\\looseness=-1\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training-data analysis. Membership inference approaches use token-level log-likelihood statistics \\citep{shi2024mink} or statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} to flag items likely seen during training. A standard validation practice is to compare detector output against web-overlap labels \\citep{li2024contamination}, which classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}---whether an item exists in training data---rather than \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts. This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our comparison with web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\n\\looseness=-1\nMeta-evaluation of static benchmark quality~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} assesses properties at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items without replacement; DIF monitoring identifies which existing items remain measurement-invariant. Dynamic benchmarks~\\citep{kiela2021dynabench} mitigate contamination by construction but lack psychometric equating. Broader surveys~\\citep{chang2024survey} identify benchmark saturation as a systemic challenge that DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }
  },
